## Question 197 (Year: 2014 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) An athlete participates in Olympics for personal triumph and nation’s glory; victors are showered with cash incentives by various agencies, on their return. Discuss the merit of state sponsored talent hunt and its cultivation as against the rationale of a reward mechanism as encouragement. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2014-gs2-q197-superkalam**

Recent government initiatives like the **IIM Amendment Bill 2023** have reignited debates about balancing institutional autonomy with public accountability in India's premier educational institutions.

## Arguments for Retaining Premier Status and Independence

### Academic Excellence and Innovation

- **Curriculum Flexibility**: Autonomous institutions can rapidly adapt to emerging technologies like AI, blockchain, and quantum computing
- **Industry-Responsive Programs**: **IIT Delhi's School of AI** launched specialized programs addressing industry 4.0 requirements
- **Research Leadership**: Independent funding decisions enable breakthrough research in critical areas like renewable energy and biotechnology
- **Faculty Autonomy**: Freedom to recruit global talent and design interdisciplinary courses enhances academic quality
- **Global Partnerships**: Direct collaboration with international universities without bureaucratic delays

### Competitive Advantages

- **Merit-Based Excellence**: Autonomous selection ensures the most capable students, maintaining institutional reputation globally
- **Innovation Ecosystem**: **IIT Madras Research Park** demonstrates how autonomy fosters startup culture and innovation
- **Quick Decision Making**: Rapid response to industry needs and technological changes without government approval delays
- **Resource Optimization**: Efficient allocation of funds based on institutional priorities rather than centralized mandates
- **Brand Value**: Premier status attracts top faculty, students, and industry partnerships

## Challenges of Enhanced Autonomy

### Equity and Access Concerns

- **Increased Fees**: Autonomous fee structure may exclude economically disadvantaged students despite **Article 21A** provisions
- **Geographic Disparities**: Premier institutions concentrated in urban areas, limiting rural access
- **Social Justice**: Risk of reducing diversity if merit-only criteria overlook historical disadvantages
- **Language Barriers**: English-medium instruction may disadvantage vernacular language students
- **Infrastructure Gaps**: Focus on premier institutions might neglect overall educational infrastructure

### Accountability Issues

- **Public Fund Utilization**: Institutions receiving taxpayer funding must remain accountable to public interest
- **Reservation Policies**: Autonomous selection might conflict with constitutionally mandated **SC/ST reservations** - **Democratic Oversight**: Reduced government control may limit legislative oversight of public institutions
- **National Priorities**: Independent course design might not align with national development goals
- **Transparency**: Less government oversight could reduce transparency in admissions and operations

## Balanced Reform Framework

- **Academic Freedom**: Complete autonomy in curriculum design and research priorities
- **Regulated Admissions**: Merit-based selection within constitutional reservation framework
- **Financial Transparency**: Independent fee structure with mandatory disclosure and need-based scholarships
- **Social Responsibility**: Mandatory rural outreach and inclusive education programs
- **Performance Metrics**: Regular assessment based on research output, placement records, and social impact

Premier institutions should retain their status with enhanced autonomy while ensuring social equity through the **National Education Policy 2020** framework. This balanced approach can maintain academic excellence while fulfilling constitutional obligations, creating a model for **India's journey toward becoming a knowledge economy by 2047**.

[Question ID: 2014-gs2-q197-superkalam]

---

## Question 198 (Year: 2014 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) Should the premier institutes like IITs/IIMs be allowed to retain premier status, allow more academic independence in designing courses and also decide mode/criteria of selection of students. Discuss in light of the growing challenges. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2014-gs2-q198-superkalam**

The Olympic journey embodies both personal achievement and national representation. Recent policy shifts emphasize proactive talent development over reactive rewards for sustainable sporting excellence.

## Merit of State-Sponsored Talent Hunt and Cultivation

- **Systematic Identification and Development** - **Khelo India Scheme**: Identifies over 3,000 athletes annually across age groups 10-23, providing structured pathways from grassroots to elite levels
 - **Target Olympic Podium Scheme (TOPS)**: Currently supports 327 elite athletes with ₹220 crore allocation for Tokyo and Paris Olympics preparation
 - **Sports Authority of India (SAI)**: Operates 68 training centers nationwide, ensuring geographic accessibility to quality coaching

- **Long-term Investment Benefits** - **Scientific Training Methods**: Integration of sports science, nutrition, and psychology from early stages maximizes athlete potential
 - **Infrastructure Development**: Creation of world-class facilities like National Centre of Excellence in various sports disciplines
 - **Educational Integration**: Schemes like Sports Integrated Education ensure academic continuity alongside sports training

- **Sustainable Ecosystem Creation** - **Coaching Excellence**: Programs like National Coaching Certification create qualified trainer networks across India
 - **Technology Integration**: Use of AI-based talent identification and performance analytics in modern training methodologies
 - **International Exposure**: Systematic overseas training and competition exposure through government funding

| **Aspect** | **Talent Hunt Model** | **Reward Mechanism** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Timing** | Proactive (10-15 years before peak) | Reactive (post-achievement) |
| **Impact** | Systematic development of multiple athletes | Individual recognition |
| **Sustainability** | Creates sporting infrastructure | Temporary motivation |
| **Cost-effectiveness** | Higher upfront, better ROI | Lower cost, limited impact |

## Rationale of Reward Mechanism

- **Recognition and Motivation** - **Cash Incentives**: Haryana's ₹6 crore for Olympic gold, Delhi's ₹3 crore demonstrate state commitment to excellence
 - **Career Security**: Government job guarantees and pension schemes provide post-retirement security for medal winners
 - **Social Status Enhancement**: Awards like Khel Ratna and Arjuna Awards elevate athlete's societal standing

- **Limitations of Reward-Only Approach** - **Delayed Impact**: Benefits accrue only after success, missing crucial developmental phases
 - **Limited Reach**: Rewards benefit few successful athletes while neglecting broader talent pool
 - **Sustainability Issues**: One-time rewards don't create lasting sporting infrastructure or culture

## Optimal Integration Strategy - Balanced Resource Allocation - 80-20 Model: Allocate 80% resources to talent development, 20% to reward mechanisms
- **Performance Milestones**: Implement graduated rewards during training phases, not just final achievements
- **Infrastructure Focus**: Prioritize facility creation over individual cash rewards for long-term impact

The **Mission Olympic Cell's** integrated approach combining TOPS with Khelo India demonstrates effectiveness of systematic talent cultivation. While rewards celebrate achievement, sustained investment in talent identification and development creates the foundation for consistent Olympic success, as evidenced by China's systematic approach yielding consistent medal tallies.

[Question ID: 2014-gs2-q198-superkalam]

---

## Question 199 (Year: 2014 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) Two parallel run schemes of the Government, viz the Adhar card and NPR, one of voluntary and the other as compulsory, have led to debates at national levels and also litigations. On merits , discuss whether or not both schemes need to run concurrently. Analyse the potential of the schemes to achieve development benefits and equitable growth. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2014-gs2-q199-superkalam**

The concurrent operation of **Aadhaar** (voluntary biometric identity) and **NPR** (compulsory population register) has generated constitutional debates and Supreme Court litigations, raising questions about their overlapping functions and developmental necessity.

## Need for Concurrent Implementation

### Distinct Functional Purposes

- **Aadhaar**: Provides **unique biometric identification** for service delivery and authentication
- **NPR**: Creates **comprehensive demographic database** capturing residence patterns, occupation, and migration data
- **Complementary Scope**: NPR covers broader population characteristics while Aadhaar focuses on identity verification
- **Policy Planning**: NPR data enables evidence-based governance while Aadhaar ensures targeted benefit delivery
- **Constitutional Mandate**: NPR fulfills Census Act 1948 requirements while Aadhaar serves welfare delivery under targeted statutes

### Administrative Synergies

- **Data Verification**: Cross-referencing prevents duplicate identities and fraudulent claims
- **Resource Optimization**: Shared infrastructure reduces long-term operational costs
- **Implementation Efficiency**: Integrated systems streamline government service delivery
- **Digital Governance**: Both contribute to **Digital India** mission through complementary data architecture
- **Security Framework**: Combined databases enhance national security and border management

## Arguments Against Concurrent Operation

### Operational Redundancies

- **Resource Duplication**: Similar data collection processes create administrative overlap
- **Implementation Burden**: Double enrollment processes strain government machinery and citizen time
- **Technical Challenges**: Multiple systems increase cybersecurity vulnerabilities and data breach risks
- **Digital Divide**: Concurrent schemes exclude digitally illiterate and marginalized communities
- **Privacy Concerns**: Extensive data collection raises surveillance and constitutional privacy issues

| **Comparison Table** | |
| --- | --- |
| **Aspect** | **Aadhaar vs NPR** |
| **Legal Basis** | Voluntary (Aadhaar Act 2016) vs Mandatory (Census Act 1948) |
| **Data Scope** | Biometric + Basic Demographics vs Comprehensive Population Data |
| **Primary Use** | Service Delivery Authentication vs Policy Planning Database |
| **Coverage** | Residents seeking services vs All usual residents |
| **Update Mechanism** | Individual-driven vs Periodic government surveys |

## Development Benefits and Equitable Growth

### Socio-Economic Impact

- **Direct Benefit Transfer**: **₹7.3 lakh crore** transferred through Aadhaar-enabled payments (2024-25), reducing leakages by **2.25%** - **Financial Inclusion**: **52 crore** Jan Dhan accounts linked with Aadhaar enabling banking access for rural poor
- **Educational Equity**: Streamlined scholarship distribution reaching **4.1 crore** students through integrated systems
- **Healthcare Access**: **Ayushman Bharat** beneficiaries identified through combined demographic and biometric verification
- **Rural Development**: **MGNREGA** payments efficiency improved by **15%** through Aadhaar-NPR integration

### Governance Transformation

- **Evidence-based Policies**: NPR demographic analysis informs targeted interventions for backward regions
- **Administrative Efficiency**: Reduced processing time for government services from weeks to minutes
- **Corruption Reduction**: **PM-KISAN** scheme leakages decreased by **18%** through biometric authentication
- **Social Justice**: Better identification and inclusion of **SC/ST** communities in welfare schemes
- **Urban Planning**: Migration patterns from NPR data inform infrastructure development in growing cities

The concurrent operation serves India's federal governance needs effectively. While operational challenges exist, the **Supreme Court's 2019 judgment** and recent **Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023** provide legal frameworks balancing development benefits with privacy concerns, making both schemes essential for achieving inclusive growth.

[Question ID: 2014-gs2-q199-superkalam]

## Question 200 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) An electronic cash transfer system for welfare schemes is an ambitious project to minimize corruption, eliminate wastage, and facilitate reforms. Comment. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2013-gs2-q200-superkalam**

India's electronic cash transfer system represents a transformative approach in welfare delivery, utilizing technology to ensure transparent and efficient benefit disbursement. With over 400 schemes digitized under the **Direct Benefit Transfer (DBT)** framework, this system has fundamentally changed how welfare reaches beneficiaries.

## Minimizing Corruption Through Electronic Transfers

- **Elimination of intermediaries**: Direct bank account transfers remove middlemen who traditionally siphoned funds meant for beneficiaries
- **Real-time tracking**: Digital systems provide complete audit trails, making every transaction traceable and accountable
- **Ghost beneficiary removal**: Aadhaar-based authentication has eliminated over **8 crore duplicate/fake beneficiaries** from welfare databases
- **Transparent fund flow**: **Public Financial Management System (PFMS)** enables real-time monitoring of government expenditure
- **Reduced discretionary power**: Automated systems limit human intervention, reducing opportunities for rent-seeking behavior

*Example*: Mahatma Gandhi NREGS saw leakage reduction from 40% to less than 10% after DBT implementation in 2015.

## Eliminating Wastage and Improving Efficiency

- **Targeted delivery**: **JAM Trinity** (Jan Dhan-Aadhaar-Mobile) ensures benefits reach only eligible beneficiaries
- **Cost reduction**: Administrative expenses decreased by **30-40%** as manual processes were eliminated
- **Faster disbursement**: Processing time reduced from weeks to hours for most welfare schemes
- **Accurate beneficiary identification**: Biometric authentication prevents multiple enrollments and ensures rightful recipients
- **Resource optimization**: Government saved **₹3.48 lakh crore** cumulatively through reduced leakages and improved targeting

*Example*: LPG subsidy through **PAHAL scheme** became world's largest cash transfer program, saving ₹50,000 crore annually.

## Facilitating Structural Reforms

- **Financial inclusion**: **PM Jan Dhan Yojana** opened 460 million bank accounts, bringing previously excluded populations into formal banking
- **Digital literacy promotion**: Beneficiaries gain familiarity with digital financial services through regular transactions
- **Data-driven policymaking**: Rich transactional data enables evidence-based policy formulation and course corrections
- **Scheme rationalization**: Digital platforms facilitate merger of overlapping schemes and elimination of redundant programs
- **Accountability enhancement**: **Grievance redressal mechanisms** through digital platforms improve citizen-government interface

The electronic cash transfer system has proven to be a paradigm shift in welfare delivery, demonstrating technology's potential to transform governance. Continued focus on digital infrastructure development and capacity building will further strengthen this foundation for inclusive growth.

[Question ID: 2013-gs2-q200-superkalam]

---

## Question 201 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) Identify the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) that are related to health.Discuss the success of the actions taken by the Government to achieve the same. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2013-gs2-q201-superkalam**

The Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) were established by the UN in 2000 to address global poverty and development challenges by 2015. Three of the eight MDGs specifically targeted health improvements.

## Health-Related MDGs

### MDG 4: Reduce Child Mortality

- **Target**: Reduce Under-Five Mortality Rate (U5MR) by two-thirds between 1990-2015
- **Focus**: Infant and child survival, immunization coverage
- **Key Indicators**: U5MR, Infant Mortality Rate (IMR), immunization rates
- **Scope**: Addressing pneumonia, diarrhea, measles, and neonatal complications
- **Timeline**: 25-year reduction target from baseline 1990 data

### MDG 5: Improve Maternal Health

- **Target**: Reduce Maternal Mortality Ratio (MMR) by three-quarters
- **Focus**: Safe childbirth, skilled birth attendance, reproductive health
- **Key Indicators**: MMR, skilled birth attendance, contraceptive prevalence
- **Scope**: Addressing hemorrhage, sepsis, unsafe abortion complications
- **Additional**: Universal access to reproductive health services

### MDG 6: Combat HIV/AIDS, Malaria and Other Diseases

- **Target**: Halt and reverse spread of major diseases
- **Focus**: HIV/AIDS prevention, malaria control, tuberculosis treatment
- **Key Indicators**: HIV prevalence, malaria incidence, TB detection rates
- **Scope**: Disease prevention, treatment access, awareness campaigns
- **Additional**: Access to essential medicines and treatment

## Government Success in Achieving Health MDGs

| **MDG** | **Achievement** | **Key Performance** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Child Mortality** | **Exceeded Target** | 78% reduction in U5MR vs 67% target |
| **Maternal Health** | **Significant Progress** | 86% reduction in MMR (1990-2015) |
| **Disease Control** | **Mixed Results** | HIV stabilized, malaria reduced, TB challenges remain |

### Major Government Initiatives Child Health Programs: - Mission Indradhanush (2014): Achieved 90% immunization coverage
- **Integrated Child Development Services (ICDS)**: Nutrition and healthcare for 10 crore children
- **Rashtriya Bal Swasthya Karyakram**: Early detection and treatment of diseases
- **Janani Shishu Suraksha Karyakram**: Free delivery and transport services **Maternal Health Interventions:** - **Janani Suraksha Yojana (2005)**: Cash incentives for institutional deliveries increased from 39% to 89%
- **Pradhan Mantri Surakshit Matritva Abhiyan**: Monthly checkups for pregnant women
- **Skilled Birth Attendant Training**: Trained over 2.5 lakh healthcare workers **Disease Control Programs:** - **National AIDS Control Programme**: HIV prevalence reduced from 0.34% to 0.22% (2017)
- **National Vector Borne Disease Control Programme**: Malaria deaths decreased by 60%
- **Revised National TB Control Programme**: Treatment success rate of 85%

### Current Health Status and Achievements

- **MMR**: Declined from 556 (1990) to 97 per 100,000 live births (2018-20)
- **IMR**: Reduced from 80 (1990) to 28 per 1000 live births (2020)
- **U5MR**: Decreased from 126 (1990) to 35 per 1000 live births (2020)
- **Life Expectancy**: Increased from 58 years (1990) to 70 years (2020)

India's success in health MDGs demonstrates effective policy implementation and healthcare system strengthening, providing a strong foundation for achieving **SDG-3** (Good Health and Well-being) by 2030.

[Question ID: 2013-gs2-q201-superkalam]

---

## Question 202 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) The basis of providing urban amenities in rural areas (PURA) is rooted in establishing connectivity. Comment. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2013-gs2-q202-superkalam**

PURA represents a transformative approach where connectivity acts as the fundamental catalyst for bridging rural-urban development gaps and creating self-sustaining rural economies.

## Physical Connectivity Infrastructure

- **Road Networks**: Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana (PMGSY) Phase IV connects 25,000 villages with all-weather roads by 2024-25, enabling market access and service delivery
- **Transportation Systems**: Enhanced mobility reduces time-distance barriers, facilitating movement of goods, services, and people between rural and urban centers
- **Last-Mile Connectivity**: Village roads connect to block, district, and state highways, integrating rural areas into broader economic networks
- **Market Linkages**: Physical connectivity enables farmers to access urban markets directly, reducing intermediary dependence and increasing income
- **Industrial Access**: Better roads facilitate establishment of agro-processing units and small-scale industries in rural areas

## Digital Connectivity Framework

- **Broadband Infrastructure**: BharatNet project connects over 2.14 lakh gram panchayats with high-speed internet, enabling digital services
- **Mobile Networks**: 96.7% mobile coverage and 95.5% 4G connectivity facilitate digital payments, e-commerce, and online education
- **E-Governance Services**: Digital connectivity enables direct benefit transfers, online certificate services, and transparent governance
- **Telemedicine Access**: Rural health centers connect to urban hospitals through telemedicine, improving healthcare delivery
- **Digital Literacy**: Connectivity supports skill development programs and online learning platforms

## Energy Connectivity Impact

- **Rural Electrification**: Saubhagya scheme electrified 28.6 million households, providing foundation for modern amenities and industrial development
- **Agricultural Mechanization**: Reliable power supply enables pump irrigation, cold storage, and food processing units
- **Small Industries**: Energy connectivity supports establishment of rural BPO centers, handicraft units, and service sectors
- **Quality of Life**: Electricity access improves education, healthcare, and communication facilities
- **Economic Opportunities**: Power availability enables home-based enterprises and skill-based industries

## Socio-Economic Transformation

- **Income Generation**: Connectivity creates diverse livelihood opportunities beyond agriculture through rural entrepreneurship and value addition
- **Service Access**: Healthcare, education, banking, and administrative services become accessible through various connectivity modes
- **Migration Control**: Enhanced amenities reduce rural-urban migration by providing urban-like facilities in rural areas
- **Women Empowerment**: Digital connectivity enables women's participation in online businesses and skill development programs
- **Youth Engagement**: Connectivity attracts educated youth to remain in villages through technology-enabled opportunities

Connectivity thus forms the backbone of PURA by creating an ecosystem where rural areas can access urban amenities while maintaining their cultural identity, contributing to India's vision of balanced regional development.

[Question ID: 2013-gs2-q202-superkalam]

## Question 203 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) The Central Government frequently complains about the poor performance of the State Governments in eradicating the suffering of the vulnerable sections of the society. Restructuring of Centrally sponsored schemes across the sectors for ameliorating the cause of vulnerable sections of the population aims at providing flexibility to the States in better implementation. Critically evaluate. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2013-gs2-q203-superkalam**

Recent **CSS restructuring initiatives** aim to address persistent implementation gaps in welfare schemes, with states like Kerala achieving 95% coverage in social security while others lag below 60%, highlighting the need for flexible, state-specific approaches.

## Benefits of CSS Restructuring

- **Scheme Rationalization**: Reduction from **66 to 30 schemes** eliminated overlapping programs and simplified administrative processes for better coordination.
- **Enhanced State Autonomy**: States can now adapt schemes to local conditions, as seen in **MGNREGA's** state-specific wage rates and Tamil Nadu's innovative work categories.
- **Financial Flexibility**: **Flexi-funds (up to 25%)** allow states to address emerging needs and local priorities within broader scheme objectives.
- **Outcome-Based Approach**: Shift from input-based to outcome-based monitoring enables focus on actual impact rather than mere expenditure.
- **Reduced Administrative Burden**: Umbrella schemes like **Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana** consolidate multiple agriculture schemes, reducing paperwork and delays.

## Implementation Challenges

- **Capacity Constraints**: Many states lack adequate institutional infrastructure and trained personnel for effective scheme management and monitoring.
- **Funding Delays**: States frequently complain about delayed central releases and burden of matching contributions, affecting timely implementation.
- **Political Differences**: Center-state political misalignment often leads to poor coordination and blame-shifting rather than collaborative problem-solving.
- **Monitoring Gaps**: Weak data collection systems and absence of real-time tracking mechanisms hamper effective performance assessment.
- **Beneficiary Targeting**: Poor identification processes and outdated databases lead to exclusion errors, particularly affecting **SC/ST** and marginalized communities.

| Aspect | Before Restructuring | After Restructuring |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Number of Schemes | 66 scattered schemes | 30 rationalized schemes |
| State Flexibility | Limited adaptation | 25% flexi-fund provision |
| Monitoring Focus | Input-based tracking | Outcome-based assessment |

## Way Forward

- **Institutional Strengthening**: Building state capacity through **National Institute of Public Administration** training programs and technical assistance.
- **Technology Integration**: Leveraging **PM-KISAN** model of direct transfers and real-time monitoring for transparent service delivery.
- **Cooperative Federalism**: Strengthening **Inter-State Council** mechanisms and **NITI Aayog's** Development Support Services for better coordination.

While CSS restructuring provides necessary flexibility, success depends on addressing institutional capacity gaps and ensuring genuine center-state partnership. The approach aligns with **Article 263** principles of cooperative governance, requiring sustained commitment from both levels of government for effective implementation.

[Question ID: 2013-gs2-q203-superkalam]

## Question 204 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) The concept of the Mid-Day Meal (MDM) scheme is almost a century old in India with early beginnings in the Madras Presidency in pre-independent India. The scheme has again been given impetus in most states in the last two decades. Critically examine its twin objectives, latest mandates, and success. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2013-gs2-q204-superkalam**

The Mid-Day Meal (MDM) scheme, now known as **PM POSHAN**, represents one of India's pioneering initiatives in addressing both educational and nutritional challenges. Starting from the **Madras Presidency** in the 1920s, the program has evolved to become the world's largest school meal program.

## Twin Objectives of MDM Scheme

- **Educational Enhancement** - Increasing **school enrollment**, particularly for marginalized sections and girl children
 - Improving **retention rates** and reducing dropout rates through nutritional incentives
 - Enhancing **classroom attendance** and academic performance through better nutrition
 - Addressing **gender disparities** by encouraging girl child education
 - Supporting **universal elementary education** goals under RTE Act 2009

- **Nutritional Security** - Addressing **classroom hunger** and malnutrition among school-going children
 - Providing minimum **one-third of daily caloric requirements** through hot-cooked meals
 - Supporting **early childhood development** through balanced nutrition
 - Combating **micronutrient deficiencies** through fortified foods
 - Breaking the **intergenerational cycle of malnutrition** ## Latest Mandates and Implementation Framework

| Component | Latest Mandate | Implementation Details |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Coverage** | PM POSHAN (2021-26) | 11.2 lakh schools, 11.8 crore children |
| **Nutrition Norms** | Enhanced calorie/protein requirements | Pre-primary: 450 kcal, Primary: 450 kcal, Upper Primary: 700 kcal |
| **Quality Assurance** | FSSAI standards | Mandatory testing, social audits |
| **Technology Integration** | Digital monitoring | Real-time tracking, GPS-enabled delivery |

- **Recent Policy Changes**:

 - Integration of **nutrition gardens** in schools to promote dietary diversity
 - Implementation of **blockchain technology** for supply chain transparency
 - Introduction of **DBT mechanism** for cost optimization
 - Inclusion of **supplementary nutrition** for pre-primary children
 - Mandatory **social audit mechanisms** for accountability

## Critical Assessment of Success

- **Quantitative Achievements**:

 - **10.4 crore children** benefiting across states/UTs (2023-24 data)
 - **63% increase** in girl child enrollment in participating schools
 - Creation of **26 lakh employment opportunities** for women cook-cum-helpers
 - **15% reduction** in malnutrition rates among beneficiary children

- **Persistent Challenges**:

 - **Quality inconsistencies** in food preparation and nutritional value
 - **Infrastructure gaps** in kitchen facilities and storage systems
 - **Supply chain disruptions** affecting regular meal provision
 - **Caste-based discrimination** in meal preparation and serving
 - **Inadequate monitoring** leading to fund misutilization

The transformation into **PM POSHAN** demonstrates India's commitment to achieving **SDG-2** and **SDG-4**. However, addressing implementation challenges through enhanced monitoring, community participation, and technological integration remains crucial for realizing the scheme's full potential in child development.

[Question ID: 2013-gs2-q204-superkalam]

## Question 205 (Year: 2013 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) The product diversification of financial institutions and insurance companies, resulting in overlapping of products and services strengthens the case for the merger of the two regulatory agencies, namely SEBI and IRDA. Justify. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2013-gs2-q205-superkalam**

Recent developments in India's financial sector show increasing convergence between banking, insurance, and securities products, with institutions like HDFC offering both mutual funds and insurance policies under one roof.

## Case for SEBI-IRDA Merger

### Product Convergence and Regulatory Overlap

- **Hybrid Products**: ULIPs combine insurance with investment features, creating jurisdictional disputes between SEBI and IRDA
- **Bancassurance Growth**: Banks now distribute insurance products alongside securities, requiring dual regulatory compliance
- **Digital Platforms**: Fintech companies offer integrated financial services spanning both regulators' domains
- **Pension Products**: Market-linked pension schemes blur boundaries between insurance and securities regulation
- **Regulatory Confusion**: The 2010 ULIP controversy demonstrated how dual regulation creates market uncertainty

<p align="center"><img src="images/superkalam_social_justice_q998_img1.jpeg" alt="Financial Product Convergence showing overlap between securities, insurance, and banking products" /></p>

Financial Product Convergence showing overlap between securities, insurance, and banking products

### Operational Efficiency Benefits

| Aspect | Current System | Merged Regulator |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Approval Process | Dual clearance required | Single-window clearance |
| Compliance Cost | Higher due to multiple regulations | Reduced compliance burden |
| Market Oversight | Fragmented supervision | Unified risk assessment |
| Consumer Protection | Different standards | Consistent protection norms |

### Global Best Practices

- **UK's FCA Model**: Successfully regulates diverse financial products under single authority since 2013
- **Australia's ASIC**: Unified regulation of corporations, financial markets, and consumer credit
- **Singapore's MAS**: Integrated supervision of banking, insurance, and securities sectors
- **Regulatory Efficiency**: Studies show 15-20% cost reduction in unified regulatory systems
- **Market Development**: Integrated regulators facilitate faster product innovation and market growth

### Implementation Framework

- **Phased Integration**: Start with overlapping product categories like ULIPs and pension schemes
- **Specialized Divisions**: Create insurance and securities wings within unified regulator
- **Legislative Support**: Amend SEBI Act, IRDA Act to enable structural integration
- **Stakeholder Consultation**: Involve industry players in designing new regulatory framework
- **International Cooperation**: Leverage expertise from countries with unified financial regulators

The merger would align India's regulatory structure with global trends while addressing current inefficiencies. This integration would strengthen financial market supervision, reduce regulatory arbitrage, and enhance consumer protection across all financial products, supporting India's vision of becoming a $5 trillion economy.

[Question ID: 2013-gs2-q205-superkalam]

## Question 206 (Year: 2025 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A)
**Question ID: 2025-gs2-q206-superkalam**

**&quot;Energy security constitutes the dominant kingpin of India&#x27;s foreign policy, and is linked with India&#x27;s overarching influence in Middle Eastern countries.&quot; How would you integrate energy security with India&#x27;s foreign policy trajectories in the coming years?**

### Answer

India's energy import dependence of **85% crude oil** necessitates strategic energy diplomacy, particularly with Middle Eastern nations accounting for over **60% of imports**.

<p align="center"><img src="images/superkalam_international_relations_q29_img1.png" alt="India's Energy Security Foreign Policy Factors" /></p>

India's Energy Security Foreign Policy Factors

## Energy Security as India's Foreign Policy Driver

- **Strategic Import Dependence**: Middle East supplies **65% of India's crude oil** with Saudi Arabia, Iraq, and UAE as top suppliers, making energy security central to regional diplomacy
- **Infrastructure Investments**: India's **$13 billion investment** in Iran's Chabahar Port and participation in **Kashagan oil field** in Kazakhstan demonstrate energy-driven foreign policy
- **Bilateral Energy Partnerships**: Recent **India-UAE CEPA 2022** includes energy cooperation worth **$75 billion**, while **India-Saudi Arabia Strategic Partnership** encompasses renewable energy collaboration
- **Diversification Imperative**: Russia's emergence as **2nd largest oil supplier** (2023) showcases India's multi-alignment strategy in energy sourcing
- **Regional Influence**: India's membership in **IEA (2017)** and **GECF observer status** enhances its voice in global energy governance

## Integration Strategies for Future Trajectories

- **Green Energy Diplomacy**:

 - **India-Middle East Green Hydrogen Alliance** with UAE targeting **5 million tonnes production by 2030** - Solar manufacturing partnerships through **One Sun One World One Grid initiative** - Joint renewable energy projects like **Mohammed bin Rashid Al Maktoum Solar Park Phase V** - **Strategic Infrastructure Development**:

 - **India-Middle East-Europe Economic Corridor (IMEC)** launched at **G20 2023** for energy connectivity
 - Participation in **Trans-Arabian Pipeline** modernization projects
 - Development of **floating LNG terminals** in partnership with Qatar and UAE

- **Technology and Investment Cooperation**:

 - **ONGC Videsh** investments exceeding **$15 billion** across Middle East and Central Asia
 - Nuclear cooperation expansion through **Kudankulam Units 5-6** with Russian technology
 - Critical minerals partnerships for **lithium and cobalt** supply chains

- **Multi-lateral Energy Frameworks**:

 - Active participation in **International Solar Alliance** with Middle Eastern nations
 - **Shanghai Cooperation Organization** energy working groups
 - **BRICS energy cooperation** mechanism for alternative payment systems

India's energy security integration with foreign policy requires balancing traditional hydrocarbon partnerships with emerging renewable collaborations, exemplified by initiatives like **National Green Hydrogen Mission 2023** and strategic petroleum reserves expansion.

## Question 207 (Year: 2025 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) India-Africa digital partnership is achieving mutual respect, co-development and long-term institutional partnerships. Elaborate. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2025-gs2-q207-superkalam**

Africa and India Partnership 

## Achieving Mutual Respect through Digital Cooperation

- **Demand-driven approach**: India avoids imposing solutions, instead responding to specific African needs like **Morocco's request for MOSIP digital identity system**.
- **Sovereignty preservation**: African nations retain control over their digital infrastructure while benefiting from Indian expertise.
- **Cultural sensitivity**: Projects like **Ghana's e-governance platform** incorporate local languages and administrative practices.
- **Equal partnership**: Both regions contribute expertise - Africa provides market insights while India shares technological solutions.
- **Capacity building focus**: Training programs ensure Africans lead their own digital transformation initiatives.

## Co-Development Models in Action

- **Joint innovation**: **Pan-African e-Network Project** connects 53 African countries through shared tele-medicine and tele-education platforms.
- **Technology adaptation**: **UPI model** customized for **Namibian payment systems** considering local banking infrastructure.
- **Collaborative funding**: India's **$12 billion concessional credit** combined with African government investments for sustainable project financing.
- **Knowledge exchange**: **IIT Madras Zanzibar campus** facilitates two-way learning between Indian and African students.
- **Research partnerships**: Joint R&D initiatives in **AI and blockchain** through institutions like **Indian Statistical Institute**.

| MOSIP Implementation | Morocco, Togo, Guinea | 2021-2025 | 50 million+ digital identities |
| Smart City Projects | Nigeria, Kenya, Rwanda | 2020-2030 | Urban digitization |

- **Sustained engagement**: **Smart Zambia Initiative (2023)** extends beyond technology transfer to governance transformation
- **Educational foundations**: 3,000+ annual scholarships for African students in Indian technical institutions
- **Institutional frameworks**: Regular **India-Africa Summit** mechanisms ensure continuous partnership evolution

This partnership exemplifies **"Atmanirbhar Bharat"** principles globally, with initiatives like **MOSIP in West Africa** and **digital agriculture platforms in East Africa** creating sustainable technological ecosystems.

## Question 208 (Year: 2025 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) &quot;The reform process in the United Nations remains unresolved, because of the delicate imbalance of East and West and entanglement of the USA vs. Russo-Chinese alliance.&quot; Examine and critically evaluate the East-West policy confrontations in this regard. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2025-gs2-q208-superkalam**

The United Nations, established in 1945, was designed to maintain international peace, security, and cooperation. Over time, the need for reform—particularly of the Security Council—has become urgent to reflect contemporary global realities.

<p align="center"><img src="images/superkalam_international_relations_q37_img1.png" alt="Stalled UN Reforms between Western and Eastern Blocs" /></p>

Stalled UN Reforms between Western and Eastern Blocs

However, UN reforms remain stalled largely due to the **East-West geopolitical divide**, reflecting the unresolved power equilibrium between the **USA and Western allies** versus **Russia and China** ## East-West Policy Confrontations in UN Reform

### Security Council Reform Deadlock - Western position: Expansion with Germany, Japan, India, Brazil as permanent members, emphasizing democratic credentials and financial contributions
- **Eastern bloc stance**: **Russia-China** oppose Western expansion, advocating for **African and Asian representation** without additional Western allies
- **Veto power disputes**: US blocks Russian proposals while **China** vetoes Western-backed resolutions, creating systematic paralysis
- **Regional alignments**: **BRICS nations** support multipolar representation versus **G7's** democracy-based membership criteria
- **Procedural battles**: **Article 108 amendment requirements** exploited by opposing blocs to prevent reforms

### Institutional Governance Conflicts - Budget disputes: US withholding contributions over Chinese influence in specialized agencies like WHO and UNIDO - Leadership contests: 2024 WHO Director-General election reflected East-West divisions with European vs Asian candidates - Operational mandates: Disagreements over peacekeeping missions in Mali, DRC, with Western emphasis on human rights versus Eastern sovereignty principles
- **Agency restructuring**: **Russia-China** bloc opposes Western-dominated **Peacebuilding Commission** expansion
- **Secretariat appointments**: Geographic distribution conflicts between **Western merit-based** versus **Eastern quota-based** systems

### Ideological Confrontations - Human rights vs sovereignty: US-EU promoting R2P doctrine opposed by Russia-China's non-interference stance
- **Climate governance**: **Green transition financing** disputes between developed-developing nation blocs
- **Technology governance**: **US** cyber-security proposals versus **Chinese** digital sovereignty framework
- **Development paradigms**: **Western liberal democracy promotion** conflicting with **Eastern alternative development models** - **International law interpretation**: Divergent views on **territorial integrity, self-determination**, evident in **Ukraine, Taiwan, Kashmir** contexts

The **East-West policy confrontations** fundamentally stem from competing visions of global governance—**Western multilateralism** versus **Eastern multipolarity**. Resolution requires **compromise mechanisms** like the **Alliance for Multilateralism** initiative and **regional consultation frameworks** to bridge ideological divides.

[Question ID: 2025-gs2-q208-superkalam]

---

## Question 209 (Year: 2025 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) &quot;With the waning of globalization, post-Cold War world is becoming a site of sovereign nationalism.&quot; Elucidate. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2025-gs2-q209-superkalam**

The post-Cold War era was marked by the rise of globalization, characterized by free trade, open borders, multilateralism, and liberal internationalism. However, in recent years, this global integration is losing momentum due to economic inequality, geopolitical tensions, cultural backlash, and technological disruption.

As a result, the world is witnessing a resurgence of sovereign nationalism where states prioritize national interests, borders, and identities over global cooperation.

<p align="center"><img src="images/superkalam_international_relations_q39_img1.png" alt="The Shift From Globalization to Nationalism" /></p>

The Shift From Globalization to Nationalism

### Waning of Globalization

1. **Surge in trade barriers:** The **WTO** reports that new trade-restrictive measures impacted a massive $887.7 billion of global trade in 2024.
2. **Weaponized supply chains:** Nations are restricting critical exports to stunt geopolitical rivals, such as China limiting gallium and graphite shipments.
3. **Paralyzed multilateralism:** Countries bypass global trade laws by invoking the National Security Exception under **GATT Article XXI**, rendering international dispute settlements ineffective.
4. **Capital decoupling:** Foreign Direct Investment is fracturing ideologically, with the **IMF** warning this could reduce worldwide GDP by 2.3%.
5. **Return of explicit tariffs:** The **G20 Trade Policy Factbook 2025** highlights a widespread revival of aggressive, direct tariff barriers among major economies.

### Rise of Sovereign Nationalism

1. **State-led industrial policy:** Nations aggressively localize manufacturing, visible in the US **CHIPS and Science Act** aiming for semiconductor supremacy.
2. **Green protectionism:** Environmental mandates are leveraged for economic nationalism, seen in the EU's **Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM)** penalizing developing imports.
3. **Digital sovereignty:** States erect virtual borders to protect citizen data, reflected in India's stringent data localization regulations.
4. **Strategic autonomy:** Nations prioritize domestic needs over bloc politics, illustrated by India securing discounted Russian oil despite Western pressure.
5. **Defense indigenization:** Governments mandate local sourcing to reduce vulnerabilities, seen in India's **Atmanirbhar Bharat** defense procurement quotas.

This shift requires recalibrating toward strategic interdependence. Middle powers like India must champion a reformed multilateralism that balances sovereign developmental needs with global supply chain resilience.

[Question ID: 2025-gs2-q209-superkalam]

---

## Question 210 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) Critically analyze India&#x27;s evolving diplomatic, economic and strategic relations with the Central Asian Republics (CARs) highlighting their increasing significance in regional and global geopolitics. 

### Answer

 **Subject:** International Relations
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q210-superkalam**

Central Asia anchors India’s "Extended Neighbourhood," evolving into deep strategic integration via the **Connect Central Asia Policy**.

<p align="center"><img src="images/superkalam_international_relations_q101_img1.png" alt="Chabahar Port Central Asia Connectivity" /></p>

Chabahar Port Central Asia Connectivity

### Evolving Relations and Geopolitical Significance

1. **Diplomatic Institutionalisation:** The newly established **India-Central Asia Secretariat** anchors frameworks like the 2025 NSA Dialogue to address emerging cyber and AI threats.
2. **Strategic Connectivity:** The historic 10-year **Chabahar Port agreement (2024)** physically bypasses Pakistan, plugging India into the INSTC and elevating its geopolitical leverage.
3. **Digital Diplomacy:** India is exporting its Digital Public Infrastructure by offering **UPI technology** free of cost to build sovereign payment systems in CARs.
4. **Energy Security:** Kazakhstan dominates trade via steady uranium exports, supplemented by the new **Rare Earths Forum** to secure critical minerals for clean energy.
5. **Defence Ties:** Joint multi-domain exercises like **DUSTLIK (2025)** and India's footprint at Tajikistan's **Farkhor Air Base** cement New Delhi's role as a regional security provider.

### Critical Analysis of the Relationship

1. **Trade Asymmetry:** Bilateral trade of **$2 billion (2024-25)** remains massively eclipsed by China’s $100 billion regional footprint.
2. **Investment Deficit:** Cumulative Indian Overseas Direct Investment (2000–2024) is merely **$439 million**, indicating structurally weak private sector engagement.
3. **Logistical Barriers:** The **2025 Exim Bank Report** explicitly flags the lack of direct land access as the primary choke point restricting traditional goods exchange.
4. **Geopolitical Rivalry:** India must navigate a highly contested landscape where Russia acts as the historical security guarantor and China drives economic hegemony.
5. **Regional Instability:** Spillover risks from Afghanistan, including terror financing and radicalisation, demand constant counter-terrorism vigilance via the **Shanghai Cooperation Organisation (SCO)**.

To overcome physical barriers, India must pivot toward digital and pharmaceutical joint ventures. Leveraging the **"DAKSHIN"** capacity-building initiative will elevate New Delhi as a vital balancing pole in a multipolar Eurasia.

[Question ID: 2024-gs2-q210-superkalam]

## Question 211 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) Discuss the geopolitical and geostrategic importance of Maldives for India with a focus on global trade and energy flows. Further also discuss how this relationship affects India&#x27;s maritime security and regional stability amidst international competition? 

### Answer

 **Subject:** International Relations
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q211-superkalam**

India's relationship with Maldives holds paramount importance due to its strategic location along vital maritime trade routes in the **Indian Ocean Region (IOR)**. The recent diplomatic tensions and increasing Chinese presence have brought renewed focus on this crucial partnership.

<p align="center"><img src="images/superkalam_international_relations_q103_img1.png" alt="India and the Indian Ocean routes" /></p>

India and the Indian Ocean routes

## Geopolitical and Geostrategic Significance

- **Maritime Trade Routes**:

 - Maldives sits astride crucial Sea Lines of Communication (SLOCs) connecting East Asia with West Asia and Europe.
 - Nearly **30% of India's total trade** and **80% of oil imports** pass through nearby waters, particularly the Strait of Malacca.
 - Acts as a vital checkpoint for monitoring maritime traffic in the central Indian Ocean.

- **Energy Security**:

 - Critical for securing India's energy imports, given that India imported **234.26 million tonnes of crude oil** in 2023-24.
 - Helps protect vital energy shipments with **89% import dependency** for crude oil and **46.6%** for natural gas.
 - Strategic location enables monitoring of energy supply chains from the Persian Gulf to East Asia.

- **Strategic Significance**:

 - **Proximity**: Just 700 km from India’s Lakshadweep and 1,200 km from its mainland, Maldives lies close to India’s west coast and crucial naval installations.
 - **Chokepoint Security**: The Maldives is near the Eight Degree Channel, a vital route for India’s energy imports and international trade.
 - **Naval Outreach**: The archipelago serves as a natural outpost for India’s maritime domain awareness (MDA) in the western Indian Ocean.

<p align="center"><img src="images/superkalam_international_relations_q103_img2.jpg" alt="Geographic positions of India and Maldives" /></p>

Geographic positions of India and Maldives

## Impact on Maritime Security and Regional Stability

- **Military Cooperation**:

 - Regular joint exercises like **'Exercise Ekuverin 2025'** enhance interoperability between armed forces.
 - Strengthens counter-terrorism and humanitarian assistance capabilities.
 - Helps maintain maritime domain awareness in the region.

- **International Competition**:

 - Growing Chinese investments through **Belt and Road Initiative (BRI)** projects like:

 - **Velana International Airport** expansion.
 - **China-Maldives Friendship Bridge**.

 - Increasing Chinese economic influence raises concerns about strategic autonomy.

- **Regional Stability Challenges**:

 - Risk of militarization of the Indian Ocean.
 - Potential for debt-trap diplomacy affecting regional power dynamics.
 - Need for balanced engagement to maintain strategic equilibrium.

The evolving geopolitical landscape in Maldives demands India's careful attention through initiatives like **"SAGAR" (Security and Growth for All in the Region)** and **"Neighborhood First" policy**. The success of India's maritime strategy largely depends on maintaining strong ties with this crucial Indian Ocean partner while balancing against external influences.

[Question ID: 2024-gs2-q211-superkalam]

## Question 212 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) Terrorism has become a significant threat to global peace and security. Evaluate the effectiveness of the United Nations Security Council&#x27;s Counter Terrorism Committee (CTC) and its associated bodies in addressing and mitigating this threat at the international level. 

### Answer

 **Subject:** International Relations
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q212-superkalam**

The recent surge in **global terrorist activities**, with over 6,700 attacks reported in 2022, has highlighted the critical role of international cooperation through institutions like the **UN Counter Terrorism Committee (CTC)**. The committee, established through **UNSC Resolution 1373** after the **9/11 attacks**, serves as the cornerstone of global counter-terrorism efforts.

<p align="center"><img src="images/superkalam_international_relations_q112_img1.png" alt="UN Security Council Counter-Terrorism Organization" /></p>

UN Security Council Counter-Terrorism Organization

## Role and Objectives of the Counter-Terrorism Committee (CTC)

The CTC, established in 2001, aims to:

- Monitor implementation of anti-terrorism measures by member states.
- Facilitate technical assistance and capacity-building.
- Encourage international cooperation and sharing of best practices.
- Oversee compliance with key resolutions such as 1267, 1373, and 1540.

It is supported by the **Counter-Terrorism Executive Directorate (CTED)**, which provides expert analysis and conducts country assessments.

## Effectiveness of CTC and Associated Bodies

### Positive Achievements

- **Institutional Framework**: Successfully established a comprehensive counter-terrorism architecture through **UNSC Resolution 1373**, mandating member states to implement specific counter-terrorism measures.
- **Global Coordination**: Enhanced international cooperation through the **Counter-Terrorism Executive Directorate (CTED)**, facilitating information sharing and best practices among member states.
- **Sanctions Regime**: Implemented effective measures against terrorist organizations through **targeted sanctions** against **ISIL** and **Al-Qaeda**, regularly renewed to maintain pressure.
- **Coordination with Global Bodies**: Collaborates with **INTERPOL, FATF, ICAO**, and other multilateral institutions.
- **Capacity Building**: Provided technical assistance and training to member states, strengthening their domestic counter-terrorism capabilities.

### Challenges and Limitations

- **Lack of a universally accepted definition of terrorism**: , due to geopolitical differences, hampers cohesive action.
- **Veto politics in UNSC**: State-sponsored terrorism or violations by powerful nations often escape scrutiny.
- **Weak enforcement mechanisms**: CTC cannot impose penalties or ensure compliance independently.
- **Selective application**: For instance, efforts to list certain individuals/groups as terrorists are blocked by some nations for strategic reasons (e.g., China blocking listings under 1267).

## Way Forward for Enhanced Effectiveness

- **Strengthened Monitoring**: Implementation of robust oversight mechanisms to ensure member state compliance.
- **Technology Integration**: Enhanced use of digital tools for better threat detection and response coordination.
- **Inclusive Approach**: Greater engagement with civil society organizations and private sector stakeholders.
- **Capacity Enhancement**: Increased focus on building member states' counter-terrorism capabilities through targeted assistance programs.

While the **CTC** has established crucial frameworks for international counter-terrorism efforts, its effectiveness can be enhanced through strengthened implementation mechanisms and adaptive strategies. The success of initiatives like the **7-state randomized controlled trial** demonstrates the potential for evidence-based approaches in combating terrorism effectively.

[Question ID: 2024-gs2-q212-superkalam]

---

## Question 213 (Year: 2024 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) &quot;The West is fostering India as an alternative to reduce dependence on China&#x27;s supply chain and as a strategic ally to counter China&#x27;s political and economic dominance.&quot; Explain this statement with examples. 

### Answer

 **Subject:** International Relations
**Question ID: 2024-gs2-q213-superkalam**

The evolving global geopolitical landscape has positioned **India as a crucial alternative** to China, marked by the West's strategic pivot towards New Delhi as evidenced by increasing economic partnerships and defense cooperation.

<p align="center"><img src="images/superkalam_international_relations_q117_img1.png" alt="Western Economies' Approaches to China and India" /></p>

Western Economies' Approaches to China and India

## Why the West Seeks an Alternative to China

- **China’s Monopoly in Global Supply Chains** - China dominates critical manufacturing, including **semiconductors, rare earths, pharmaceuticals**, and electronics.
 - The COVID-19 pandemic, and supply shocks during geopolitical tensions (e.g., US-China trade war), exposed global vulnerabilities in **over-dependence on China**.

- **Strategic Assertiveness of China**:

 - Military aggression in the **South China Sea, Taiwan Strait**, and along the **India-China LAC** raises global security concerns.
 - Use of economic coercion **(e.g., against Australia)** signals China's willingness to use trade as a geopolitical tool.

## Economic Dimension

- **Trade Diversification**: Western nations are actively promoting India as an alternative manufacturing hub, reflected in the **Make in India** initiative and **Production Linked Incentive (PLI)** schemes attracting global companies.
- **Supply Chain Resilience**: The West's "China+1" strategy has led to manufacturing shifts, though the transition remains gradual with only selective companies choosing India as an alternative destination.

## Strategic and Defense Cooperation

- **Quad (India, US, Japan, Australia)**: Counter China's influence in the Indo-Pacific; focuses on maritime security, infrastructure, and technology cooperation.
- **India-US 2+2 Dialogue**: Deepens defense and strategic cooperation, including intelligence sharing and joint military exercises.
- **Indo-Pacific Economic Framework (IPEF)**: India is engaged in Indo-Pacific Economic Framework (IPEF), focusing on resilient supply chains and clean tech.

## Diplomatic Alignments

- **Indo-Pacific Strategy**: Western powers actively support India's role in maintaining a **"free, open, and inclusive Indo-Pacific"**.
- **Multilateral Forums**: Enhanced cooperation through platforms like **I2U2** (India-Israel-UAE-US) and **QUAD** demonstrates India's growing strategic importance.
- **ASEAN Centrality**: India's support for ASEAN-led mechanisms aligns with Western objectives of maintaining regional stability.

## Economic Security Initiatives

- **Critical Supply Chains**: Collaboration in **semiconductors**, **rare earth elements**, and **green technology** through initiatives like the **India-EU Trade and Technology Council**.
- **Digital Partnership**: Joint ventures in **5G technology** and **cybersecurity** to reduce dependence on Chinese tech infrastructure.

The West's strategic fostering of India as an alternative to China represents a calculated response to growing concerns about Beijing's assertiveness. Through **multi-dimensional cooperation** in trade, defense, and technology, India is emerging as a crucial partner in the **Indo-Pacific strategic framework**, though challenges in matching China's manufacturing capabilities and infrastructure development remain significant.

## Question 214 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) Indian diaspora has scaled new heights in the west. Describe its economic and political benefits for India. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2023-gs2-q214-superkalam**

The Indian diaspora, particularly in the **Western nations** such as the USA, UK, Canada, and Australia, has emerged as a highly skilled, affluent, and politically active community. Their rising prominence offers **multiple economic and political advantages** to India, making them an essential component of India’s global strategy.

<p align="center"><img src="images/superkalam_international_relations_q190_img1.png" alt="Indian Diaspora Benefits in the West" /></p>

Indian Diaspora Benefits in the West

## Economic Benefits of Indian Diaspora in the West 1. High Volume of Remittances - India received $111 billion in remittances in 2022 (World Bank), the highest globally.
 - A significant share comes from high-income countries like **USA, UK, Canada, and Australia**.
 - These remittances support household consumption, education, health, and local economies, particularly in states like **Kerala, Punjab, and Andhra Pradesh**.

2. **Foreign Direct Investment and Business Collaborations** - Diaspora professionals and business leaders invest in India’s startups, tech parks, and infrastructure.
 - Example: **Silicon Valley entrepreneurs** of Indian origin often fund Indian IT and SaaS startups.
 - Initiatives like **Overseas Citizen of India (OCI)** cardholders ease investment flows.

3. **Technology and Knowledge Transfer** - Indian-origin professionals in companies like **Google, Microsoft, and IBM** bring cutting-edge knowledge and promote tech collaborations.
 - India benefits through R&D tie-ups, hackathons, and AI/ML training programs.

4. **Promotion of Indian Goods, Culture and Tourism** - Diaspora promotes Indian exports such as **organic food, ayurvedic products, handicrafts**, etc., creating niche markets abroad.
 - **Example:** The popularity of **yoga and Indian cuisine** in the West can be attributed in part to the diaspora.

5. **Development of Education and Research Ecosystem** - Diaspora scholars support Indian universities through visiting faculty programs, curriculum reform, and funding.
 - **Example:PanIIT alumni** collaborate with Indian institutions on innovation and skilling.

6. **Boost to India's Global Service Economy** - Indian-origin professionals in top roles abroad create demand for **India-based services**, especially in **IT, fintech, and healthcare**.
 - They also facilitate **outsourcing contracts** and offshoring of operations to India.

## Political Benefits of Indian Diaspora in the West 1. Enhancing India's Soft Power and Global Image - The global success of the diaspora enhances India’s reputation as a country of talent and values.
 - **Example:** Leaders like **Kamala Harris (US VP)** and **Rishi Sunak (UK PM)** symbolize Indian civilisational depth and multiculturalism.

2. **Diaspora Diplomacy and Policy Advocacy** - Diaspora organizations lobby host governments to support India’s interests, such as **trade deals, defence cooperation**, or during crises.
 - **Example:** Indian-American groups played a role in lobbying for the **Indo-US Nuclear Deal (2008)**.

3. **People-to-People Connect Enhancing Bilateral Relations** - Cultural events, student exchanges, and diaspora-led business councils help strengthen ties.
 - **Example:India-US ties** are significantly bolstered by the **4.8 million-strong Indian-American population**.

4. **Support During Crises and Emergencies** - The diaspora actively mobilized **oxygen, medicines, and funds** during the **COVID-19 second wave** in India.
 - They also raise international awareness on natural disasters and humanitarian needs.

5. **Influencing Political Discourse in Host Countries** - Diaspora members entering politics influence foreign policy discourse toward India.
 - **Example:** Legislators of Indian origin in the **US Congress** or **UK Parliament** raise issues relevant to India, including **immigration reform** and **anti-terror support**.

6. **Building Strategic Alliances and Track-II Diplomacy** - Diaspora think tanks and advocacy groups facilitate **track-II diplomacy**, building informal channels of influence.
 - **Example:** Indian-origin academics in Western universities often contribute to shaping **India-centric narratives** in foreign policy circles.

The Indian diaspora in the West is a vital national asset. With rising political and economic capital, they serve as **catalysts of India's soft power, economic development, and diplomatic influence**. Institutional mechanisms like **Pravasi Bharatiya Divas**, **India Development Foundation of Overseas Indians**, and the **MEA's outreach programs** must be strengthened to tap this potential fully.

## Question 215 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) Sea is an important component of the Cosmos’. Discuss in the light of the above statement the role of the IMO (International Maritime Organisation) in protecting environment and enhancing maritime safety and security. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2023-gs2-q215-superkalam**

The ocean, often symbolised as a cosmic sea, embodies primordial chaos and creation myths across cultures, representing interconnected ecosystems and the balance of the planet. It regulates Earth’s climate, sustains biodiversity, and drives global trade, making its protection critical to cosmic harmony

<p align="center"><img src="images/superkalam_international_relations_q191_img1.png" alt="IMO's Three Pillars of Maritime Governance" /></p>

IMO's Three Pillars of Maritime Governance

## Role of IMO in Environmental Protection

- **Emission Reduction Strategy**: IMO has set ambitious targets to reduce shipping emissions by **30% by 2030** and **80% by 2040** compared to 2008 levels, aiming for **net-zero emissions by 2050**.
- **Marine Biodiversity Conservation**: Supporting the implementation of **BBNJ Agreement** (Biodiversity Beyond National Jurisdiction), with **22 countries ratifying** and **115 signing** the agreement as of June 2025.
- **Technical Cooperation Programme**: Allocation of **$28.4 million** for the **Integrated Technical Cooperation Programme (ITCP)** in 2024-2025 to support developing nations in environmental compliance.

## Maritime Safety Enhancement

- **SOLAS Convention Implementation**: Ensuring strict adherence to the **Safety of Life at Sea Convention** through regular inspections and certifications.
- **Port State Control**: Implementing rigorous inspection systems to verify vessels' compliance with international safety standards.
- **Capacity Building**: Supporting member states through **technical assistance** and **training programs** for maritime personnel.

## Maritime Security Measures

- **ISPS Code Enforcement**: Implementation of the **International Ship and Port Facility Security Code** to prevent terrorism and piracy.
- **Maritime Domain Awareness**: Promoting information sharing and surveillance systems for early threat detection.
- **Counter-Piracy Initiatives**: Coordinating with regional organizations like **ReCAAP** (Regional Cooperation Agreement on Combating Piracy) to combat maritime crimes.

## Challenges and Way Forward

- **Resource Constraints**: Additional funding needed beyond the current **47% coverage** of ITCP requirements.
- **Technology Integration**: Promoting digitalization and automation for enhanced monitoring and compliance.
- **Stakeholder Coordination**: Strengthening cooperation between member states, industry, and environmental organizations.

As a UN agency, the IMO fosters international cooperation on uniform safety protocols and climate action. Its focus on **search and rescue (SAR)** plans and cetacean protection routes underscores its role in preserving marine biodiversity. By aligning with **SDG 14 (“Life Below Water”)**, the IMO bridges cosmic environmental stewardship with practical governance

## Question 216 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) The expansion and strengthening of NATO and a stronger US-Europe strategic partnership works well for India. What is your opinion about this statement? Give reasons and examples to support your answer. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2023-gs2-q216-superkalam**

The **renewed strength of NATO** and the deepening **US-Europe strategic partnership**, especially after the Russia-Ukraine conflict, have reshaped global geopolitics. Though not a member, India stands to **gain strategically and diplomatically** from this evolving alignment.

<p align="center"><img src="images/superkalam_international_relations_q195_img1.png" alt="NATO, US-Europe Benefits, Strategic Security for India" /></p>

NATO, US-Europe Benefits, Strategic Security for India

## Yes, it works well for India – Reasons and Examples 1. Checks Revisionist Powers like China and Russia - NATO’s increased focus on collective security and global norms helps limit Russia's aggression in Eurasia and China's assertiveness in the Indo-Pacific.
 - **Example:** NATO’s **2022 Strategic Concept** for the first time acknowledged China as a “challenge,” aligning with India’s concerns in the Indo-Pacific.

2. **Frees Up US Focus Towards Indo-Pacific** - A more self-reliant and assertive **European security ecosystem** enables the **US to allocate more military and diplomatic bandwidth** toward the Indo-Pacific, where India faces China.
 - **Example:** US-led initiatives like **AUKUS** and **QUAD** have received more attention post NATO’s realignment.

3. **Stronger Global Rules-Based Order** - A robust US-Europe alliance pushes back against authoritarian threats and reinforces a **rules-based international order**, which aligns with India's commitment to **sovereignty and non-intervention**.
 - India benefits from the **normative weight** such coalitions bring on global governance issues like **maritime security and terrorism**.

4. **Enhanced Defence Cooperation and Technology Sharing** - Strengthened NATO cooperation encourages **defence technology transfers** and **joint exercises**, where India indirectly benefits via the **India–US and India–France strategic partnerships**.
 - **Example:** India’s acquisition of **Rafale jets from France** and **joint naval drills like Malabar and Varuna** showcase convergence on strategic interests.

5. **Support on Multilateral Platforms** - The US and Europe have increasingly supported **India’s role in global governance**—such as reforms in **UNSC and export control regimes** (MTCR, Wassenaar).
 - Their partnership enhances India’s diplomatic leverage on **climate action, technology, and global health**.

## Counter-Concerns 1. Risk of Global Polarisation and New Bloc Politics - Strengthened NATO may revive Cold War-style bipolarity, weakening multilateralism and marginalising countries like India that prefer strategic autonomy.
 - India’s non-aligned stance on conflicts like **Russia-Ukraine** may face increased pressure from both sides.

2. **Strained India-Russia Defence Ties** - NATO’s posture towards Russia could undermine India’s long-standing **defence partnership with Moscow**, which still supplies over **50% of India’s military hardware**.
 - **Example:** The **S-400 missile deal** faces the risk of **US CAATSA sanctions**, complicating India’s defence diplomacy.

3. **Pressure to Align on Foreign Policy Issues** - India may be expected to side with the West on controversial issues like **human rights**, **sanctions**, and **military interventions**, potentially compromising its **independent foreign policy**.
 - **Example:** Western unease over India’s oil imports from Russia despite global sanctions.

4. **Trade and Regulatory Challenges** - A more assertive Europe may introduce policies like the **Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM)** that disadvantage Indian exporters.
 - This could lead to **non-tariff barriers** in critical sectors such as steel, aluminium, and manufacturing.

5. **Neglect of Global South Priorities** - A NATO-focused Western alliance may underemphasize **development concerns of the Global South**, such as **climate finance**, **food security**, and **vaccine equity**, areas where India has taken global leadership.
 - **Example:** Limited Western response to India's call for **TRIPS waiver on COVID vaccines**.

6. **Possibility of Security Dilemmas in Asia** - NATO’s expanding interest in the Indo-Pacific may trigger **security dilemmas** with China, increasing regional instability.
 - India may get entangled in **great power rivalries**, complicating its engagement with ASEAN, Russia, and the Global South.

The **expansion and strengthening of NATO and transatlantic ties** broadly align with India’s **long-term strategic goals**, particularly in counterbalancing China and upholding a **rules-based order**. However, India must carefully calibrate its approach to **retain strategic autonomy**, continue engagement with Russia, and **assert its role as a balancing power** in the evolving multipolar world.

[Question ID: 2023-gs2-q216-superkalam]

## Question 217 (Year: 2023 | Paper: GS 2 | Marks: N/A) Virus of conflict is affecting the functioning of the SCO”. In the light of the above statement point out the role of India in mitigating the problems. 

### Answer
**Question ID: 2023-gs2-q217-superkalam**

The Shanghai Cooperation Organisation (SCO), a key Eurasian grouping, is increasingly strained by intra-member conflicts, particularly India-China and India-Pakistan tensions. These frictions hamper its consensus-based functioning. In this context, India plays a vital role in steering the SCO towards cooperation and constructive engagement.

<p align="center"><img src="images/superkalam_international_relations_q197_img1.png" alt="India's SECURE strategy for SCO explained" /></p>

India's SECURE strategy for SCO explained

## Virus of Conflict in the SCO: Key Challenges 1. India-China Border Tensions: - The Galwan Valley clash (2020) and subsequent military standoff have strained bilateral relations.
 - Resulted in reduced trust and limited cooperation within the SCO security framework.

2. **India-Pakistan Hostilities:** - Pakistan’s **sponsorship of cross-border terrorism** and its attempts to **internationalize bilateral issues like Kashmir** undermine the SCO’s non-interference principle.
 - Acrimonious exchanges in SCO meetings have politicized the platform.

3. **Diverging Interests of Major Players:** - **Russia-China axis** dominates the agenda, while India often resists China’s Belt and Road Initiative (BRI) which is endorsed by other members.
 - **Central Asian states** seek neutrality and development, but are caught in great power rivalries.

4. **Lack of Institutional Cohesion:** - Absence of strong dispute resolution mechanisms.
 - Differences on counter-terrorism definitions and priorities hinder **RATS (Regional Anti-Terrorist Structure)** effectiveness.

5. **Russia-West Rivalry Shifting SCO’s Strategic Focus:** - Russia’s confrontation with the West post-Ukraine war has pushed it closer to China, altering the internal power dynamics of the SCO.
 - This alignment weakens the SCO’s independent decision-making capacity and reduces space for consensus-building.
 - **Example:** SCO statements on international crises (e.g. Ukraine) remain neutral or vague due to internal divisions.

6. **Diverging Approaches to Afghanistan:** - SCO members differ sharply on dealing with the Taliban regime post-US withdrawal.
 - China and Pakistan advocate engagement for stability, whereas India and some Central Asian states demand an inclusive government and guarantees against terrorism.
 - This divergence has prevented the formulation of a coherent regional strategy on Afghanistan.

## Role of India in Mitigating SCO Conflicts India has played a constructive and multi-pronged role in countering the conflicts and enhancing cooperative spirit within SCO:

1. **Upholding Principles of Sovereignty and Non-Interference** - India consistently asserts that **bilateral disputes must not vitiate multilateral forums**.
- Refused to endorse BRI due to its violation of sovereignty through the **China-Pakistan Economic Corridor (CPEC)** in Pakistan-occupied Kashmir (PoK), but still participates constructively.

1. **Promoting Connectivity and Development sans Hegemony** - Advocates **transparent, rules-based connectivity** respecting national sovereignty.
- Highlighted initiatives like **Chabahar Port** and **International North-South Transport Corridor (INSTC)** as alternatives to Chinese infrastructure dominance.

1. **Fostering Anti-Terror Cooperation** - India emphasizes **zero tolerance** for terrorism in all forms, especially **state-sponsored** terrorism.
- Hosted the **RATS meeting in Delhi (2022)**, stressing need for common definitions and action against terror financing.

1. **Cultural and Soft Power Diplomacy** - India leverages civilizational linkages with Central Asia through **Buddhism, trade, and diaspora**.
- Organised **SCO Startup Forum and Film Festival**, projecting **soft diplomacy** and people-to-people ties.

1. **Strategic Balancing** - India’s **multi-alignment policy** balances Russia and Central Asian interests without endorsing China-led hegemony.
- Advocates for **multipolar Asia** and counters Chinese dominance diplomatically.

1. **Hosting SCO Presidency (2023)** - As SCO Chair in 2023, India:

 - Hosted over **140 meetings and events** under its presidency.
 - Focused on **digital inclusion, startups, and cooperation in agriculture, culture, and education**.
 - Excluded Pakistan from SCO Film Festival due to concerns over extremism narratives.

## Way Forward: Enhancing India’s Role 1. Promote Neutral Platforms for dialogue between adversaries like India-Pakistan and India-China.
2. Strengthen **economic cooperation** in energy, pharmaceuticals, and IT with Central Asia.
3. Lead efforts to **reform SCO structures** to include **crisis management mechanisms**.
4. Build coalitions with **like-minded members** (e.g., Russia, Central Asian Republics) to counterbalance Chinese overreach.

India’s **principled engagement** within the SCO, despite underlying conflicts, reflects its commitment to **regional stability, counterterrorism, and multipolarity**. In a conflict-prone multilateral body, India's **balanced diplomacy, economic initiatives, and normative leadership** can help mitigate tensions and make SCO a more effective regional forum.

[Question ID: 2023-gs2-q217-superkalam]
